I don’t mean to, it just comes out before I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have trouble staying still, even when you're not feeling trapped?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. Even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful, my leg keeps bouncing, or I’m tapping my fingers on the chair. Sometimes I’ll be twisting something in my hands the whole time without even really thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I mess things up sometimes, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s from missing little details or making careless mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sitting still or feel restless at work or school as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’m not working or in school right now, but I feel that way pretty much anywhere I have to sit still. Like at church, the clinic, even at home, it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day. If I have to stay seated too long, I start feeling trapped and I need to get up or do something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I got in trouble for that a lot.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have trouble paying attention or following instructions when you were a kid?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"When folks are talking through ride times, pantry lists, or church plans, I listen good at first, but then my mind slips off and I miss pieces I really needed to hear.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just feel restless all the time, like I got a motor running inside me. If I have to sit still—like at church, here at the clinic, or even just at home—I start feeling trapped, and I have to get up or do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not like I’m doing the same thing over and over because I have to. I haven’t really noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll jump in with an answer before the person’s even done talking, like the pastor, my cousin, or the lady at the pantry. Then right after, I realize I answered the wrong thing or I made them mad. I don’t mean to, it just comes out before I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have trouble staying still, even when you're not feeling trapped?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. Even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful, my leg keeps bouncing, or I’m tapping my fingers on the chair. Sometimes I’ll be twisting something in my hands the whole time without even really thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I mess things up sometimes, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s from missing little details or making careless mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sitting still or feel restless at work or school as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’m not working or in school right now, but I feel that way pretty much anywhere I have to sit still. Like at church, the clinic, even at home, it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day. If I have to stay seated too long, I start feeling trapped and I need to get up or do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or have trouble staying still when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even when I’m with friends or doing something I like, I still get that restless feeling. It’s like I’ve got a motor running in me, and if I’m sitting too long I start feeling trapped. I usually have to get up, move around, or do something with my hands.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness or impulsive behaviors are causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. People at church, at the pantry, even my family… they don’t really trust me like they used to with rides or appointments or supplies. I mean, I try to help, but I mess things up or act too quick, and then I feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish tasks or projects you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll start something, like an errand for a relative or helping with a job at the pantry, and I’ll get the first part done… but then I forget the rest of it. Later somebody asks about it and I realize I never actually finished it. It happens enough that people get upset with me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel restless when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. Back when I was a boy in school, I was always getting moved around by the teachers because I couldn’t stay in my seat. I’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to, or jump up before class was even over. I got in trouble for that a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or following instructions when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. If somebody was telling me what to do, I’d hear the first part okay, but then my mind would kind of drift off. Then I’d miss the rest of the instructions and not know what I was supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these restless feelings and impulsive behaviors your whole life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty much my whole life. I remember in school, teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put. I’d talk out of turn, jump up before class was done, and I was always getting in trouble for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. I mean, not like I’m doing the same thing over and over because I have to. I’m not working or in school right now, but I feel that way pretty much anywhere I have to sit still. Like at church, the clinic, even at home, it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day. If I have to stay seated too long, I start feeling trapped and I need to get up or do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or have trouble staying still when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even when I’m with friends or doing something I like, I still get that restless feeling. It’s like I’ve got a motor running in me, and if I’m sitting too long I start feeling trapped. I usually have to get up, move around, or do something with my hands.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness or impulsive behaviors are causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. I remember in school, teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put. People at church, at the pantry, even my family… they don’t really trust me like they used to with rides or appointments or supplies. I mean, I try to help, but I mess things up or act too quick, and then I feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish tasks or projects you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If somebody was telling me what to do, I’d hear the first part okay, but then my mind would kind of drift off. I’d talk out of turn, jump up before class was done, and I was always getting in trouble for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. It makes me feel like I keep letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, it’s caused problems with people close to me. Folks at church, the pantry, and my family don’t really trust me the same now with rides or appointments or keeping track of supplies. I want to help, but I mess it up or don’t handle it right, and then I feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your problems with restlessness and impulsivity are more bothersome to you than your problems with paying attention and following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the restless part bothers me the most. It happens enough that people get upset with me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel restless when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. Back when I was a boy in school, I was always getting moved around by the teachers because I couldn’t stay in my seat. I’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to, or jump up before class was even over. I got in trouble for that a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or following instructions when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. If somebody was telling me what to do, I’d hear the first part okay, but then my mind would kind of drift off. Then I’d miss the rest of the instructions and not know what I was supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these restless feelings and impulsive behaviors your whole life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty much my whole life. I remember in school, teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put. I’d talk out of turn, jump up before class was done, and I was always getting in trouble for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. Like I’ll start an errand for a relative, or start doing something at the pantry, and I’ll do the first part… then I get distracted or lose track of what I was supposed to finish. Later somebody will ask about it and that’s when they find out I never finished the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Have your restless feelings and impulsive behaviors caused you significant distress or problems in your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. People at church, at the pantry, even in my family… they don’t really trust me like they used to with rides or appointments or keeping track of supplies. I’ll mean to help, I really do, but then something goes wrong because I act too fast or don’t follow through right. It makes me feel like I keep letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. My papers and appointment cards get mixed up with food boxes or little notes I put in my phone, and then I can’t tell what I’m supposed to do first. By the time I figure it out, something’s already late or messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had problems with friendships or romantic relationships because of your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, it’s caused problems with people close to me. Folks at church, the pantry, and my family don’t really trust me the same now with rides or appointments or keeping track of supplies. I want to help, but I mess it up or don’t handle it right, and then I feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your problems with restlessness and impulsivity are more bothersome to you than your problems with paying attention and following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the restless part bothers me the most. It feels like I’ve got a motor running inside me all day. If I have to sit still at church, or at the clinic, or even at home, I start feeling trapped, like I need to get up or do something right then.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior when you were a kid, saying you were overly active or had trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did. When I was a boy in school, teachers were always moving my seat around because I couldn’t stay put. I’d talk out of turn, and I’d get in trouble for jumping up before class was even over.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just feel restless all the time, like I got a motor running inside me. If I have to sit still—like at church, here at the clinic, or even just at home—I start feeling trapped, and I have to get up or do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not like I’m doing the same thing over and over because I have to. I haven’t really noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll jump in with an answer before the person’s even done talking, like the pastor, my cousin, or the lady at the pantry. Then right after, I realize I answered the wrong thing or I made them mad. I don’t mean to, it just comes out before I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have trouble staying still, even when you're not feeling trapped?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. Even when I’m trying to sit there and be respectful, my leg keeps bouncing, or I’m tapping my fingers on the chair. Sometimes I’ll be twisting something in my hands the whole time without even really thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I mess things up sometimes, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s from missing little details or making careless mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sitting still or feel restless at work or school as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’m not working or in school right now, but I feel that way pretty much anywhere I have to sit still. Like at church, the clinic, even at home, it feels like I got a motor running inside me all day. If I have to stay seated too long, I start feeling trapped and I need to get up or do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or have trouble staying still when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even when I’m with friends or doing something I like, I still get that restless feeling. It’s like I’ve got a motor running in me, and if I’m sitting too long I start feeling trapped. I usually have to get up, move around, or do something with my hands.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness or impulsive behaviors are causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. People at church, at the pantry, even my family… they don’t really trust me like they used to with rides or appointments or supplies. I mean, I try to help, but I mess things up or act too quick, and then I feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to finish tasks or projects you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll start something, like an errand for a relative or helping with a job at the pantry, and I’ll get the first part done… but then I forget the rest of it. Later somebody asks about it and I realize I never actually finished it. It happens enough that people get upset with me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or feel restless when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. Back when I was a boy in school, I was always getting moved around by the teachers because I couldn’t stay in my seat. I’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to, or jump up before class was even over. I got in trouble for that a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or following instructions when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. If somebody was telling me what to do, I’d hear the first part okay, but then my mind would kind of drift off. Then I’d miss the rest of the instructions and not know what I was supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these restless feelings and impulsive behaviors your whole life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty much my whole life. I remember in school, teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put. I’d talk out of turn, jump up before class was done, and I was always getting in trouble for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. Like I’ll start an errand for a relative, or start doing something at the pantry, and I’ll do the first part… then I get distracted or lose track of what I was supposed to finish. Later somebody will ask about it and that’s when they find out I never finished the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Have your restless feelings and impulsive behaviors caused you significant distress or problems in your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. People at church, at the pantry, even in my family… they don’t really trust me like they used to with rides or appointments or keeping track of supplies. I’ll mean to help, I really do, but then something goes wrong because I act too fast or don’t follow through right. It makes me feel like I keep letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. My papers and appointment cards get mixed up with food boxes or little notes I put in my phone, and then I can’t tell what I’m supposed to do first. By the time I figure it out, something’s already late or messed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had problems with friendships or romantic relationships because of your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, it’s caused problems with people close to me. Folks at church, the pantry, and my family don’t really trust me the same now with rides or appointments or keeping track of supplies. I want to help, but I mess it up or don’t handle it right, and then I feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your problems with restlessness and impulsivity are more bothersome to you than your problems with paying attention and following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the restless part bothers me the most. It feels like I’ve got a motor running inside me all day. If I have to sit still at church, or at the clinic, or even at home, I start feeling trapped, like I need to get up or do something right then.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior when you were a kid, saying you were overly active or had trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did. I’d talk out of turn, and I’d get in trouble for jumping up before class was even over.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these restless feelings and impulsive behaviors in all areas of your life, or are they mainly in specific situations?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty much everywhere, not just one place.
